```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nAI text detection asks a single binary question---\\emph{human or machine?}---and recent methods answer it with increasing reliability \\cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.\nYet this framing elides a richer structure emerging on the open web: as LLM outputs re-enter training corpora \\cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \\emph{recursive depths}---an estimated 7--17\\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \\cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}---and each generation cycle pushes distributional properties further from both the human source and the first-generation predecessor.\nWhile most deployed LLM outputs use instruction-tuned models, base-model generation pipelines remain prevalent in synthetic data production, pre-training corpus construction, and recursive data augmentation; we accordingly scope this study to base (pretrained) models.\nKnowing \\emph{how many times} text has been recursively generated matters for data provenance \\cite{ccnet,dsir} and model collapse analysis \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}, yet no current method moves beyond the binary boundary.\n\nDistributional compression under recursive generation is well-documented \\cite{curious_decline,broken_telephone,markovian_chains}.\nWe introduce \\emph{depth fingerprinting}---classifying text by ordinal generation depth (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+)---and decompose this compression into structurally distinct components: across eight base-model generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B), the NLL distribution's central tendency (mean entropy, lexical diversity) drifts monotonically with depth while its shape (entropy extremes, tail structure) remains an order of magnitude more stable ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$ vs.\\ $d < 0.10$; convergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures confirms this partition).\nWe term this location/shape asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR)---a structural property of fixed-parameter autoregressive sampling that is mechanistically distinct from the tail-coverage erosion in model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion} and analytically informative independent of both the specific mechanism and classification accuracy.\nWhere concurrent work characterizes \\emph{that} and \\emph{why} convergence occurs \\cite{broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, DDR isolates \\emph{what} converges from what is preserved, and leverages the stationary shape component for cross-generator depth prediction without per-generator calibration.\n\nAs a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at 2.4$\\times$ lower inference cost; an end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Clustering-based validation ($k{=}2$, silhouette\\,=\\,0.660) confirms that inference-chain drift separates into a drifting location component ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and a stable shape component ($d < 0.10$). Where prior work documents \\emph{what converges}---diversity loss \\cite{curious_decline}, attractor dynamics \\cite{markovian_chains}---DDR identifies \\emph{what is preserved}: shape invariance provides the cross-generator reference needed for depth detection without per-generator calibration.\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.\n\\end{enumerate}\nA proof-of-concept classifier and systematic boundary mapping across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, and model scale establish where DDR-based detection succeeds and where it fails.\n",
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Pkag2nxr24Gir5JoM6xo9t`</summary>

```text
1	% Abstract — Depth Fingerprinting of Recursive LLM Generation Chains
2	\begin{abstract}
3	When base-model LLM outputs re-enter generation pipelines, each recursive step compresses \emph{where} text sits in distributional space while leaving \emph{how} it is shaped nearly unchanged.
4	We identify and characterize this asymmetry as \emph{differential depth response} (DDR) and characterize it across eight \textbf{base-model} generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B parameters): under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.9$, $\tau \leq 0.7$), NLL location statistics and lexical diversity drift monotonically with generation depth (Cohen's $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$), while distributional shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$)---a separation confirmed by convergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures and clustering-based validation (silhouette\,=\,0.660).
5	Where prior work on iterative generation documents \emph{what converges}, the novel observation here is \emph{what is preserved}: shape invariance under the same process that compresses location provides the stable reference needed for depth estimation without per-generator calibration.
6	As a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble exploiting DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, effective resolution: three depth classes) under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at 2.4$\times$ lower inference cost; an end-to-end classifier recovers substantially more (F1\,=\,0.905, 6-gen LOGO), establishing the analytical framework---not the specific features---as the primary contribution.
7	The signal attenuates under instruction tuning; current applicability is restricted to base models.
8	\end{abstract}
9	
```

```json
      "content": "% Abstract — Depth Fingerprinting of Recursive LLM Generation Chains\n\\begin{abstract}\nWhen base-model LLM outputs re-enter generation pipelines, each recursive step compresses \\emph{where} text sits in distributional space while leaving \\emph{how} it is shaped nearly unchanged.\nWe identify and characterize this asymmetry as \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR) and characterize it across eight \\textbf{base-model} generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B parameters): under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.9$, $\\tau \\leq 0.7$), NLL location statistics and lexical diversity drift monotonically with generation depth (Cohen's $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$), while distributional shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$)---a separation confirmed by convergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures and clustering-based validation (silhouette\\,=\\,0.660).\nWhere prior work on iterative generation documents \\emph{what converges}, the novel observation here is \\emph{what is preserved}: shape invariance under the same process that compresses location provides the stable reference needed for depth estimation without per-generator calibration.\nAs a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble exploiting DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, effective resolution: three depth classes) under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at 2.4$\\times$ lower inference cost; an end-to-end classifier recovers substantially more (F1\\,=\\,0.905, 6-gen LOGO), establishing the analytical framework---not the specific features---as the primary contribution.\nThe signal attenuates under instruction tuning; current applicability is restricted to base models.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
```

```json
{
```

```json
  "parentUuid": "bd88ab5c-2baf-48b5-8068-71ac903b3668",

  "promptId": "303a12b0-8b17-40b6-82a9-ac391d086bf0",

  "uuid": "a3c00758-04d1-445d-b1e2-381e12119948",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "matching multi-detector ensembles at 2.4$\\times$ lower inference cost",
    "newString": "surpassing individual detection baselines by 6.7--36.3\\,pp",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nAI text detection asks a single binary question---\\emph{human or machine?}---and recent methods answer it with increasing reliability \\cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.\nYet this framing elides a richer structure emerging on the open web: as LLM outputs re-enter training corpora \\cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \\emph{recursive depths}---an estimated 7--17\\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \\cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}---and each generation cycle pushes distributional properties further from both the human source and the first-generation predecessor.\nWhile most deployed LLM outputs use instruction-tuned models, base-model generation pipelines remain prevalent in synthetic data production, pre-training corpus construction, and recursive data augmentation; we accordingly scope this study to base (pretrained) models.\nKnowing \\emph{how many times} text has been recursively generated matters for data provenance \\cite{ccnet,dsir} and model collapse analysis \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}, yet no current method moves beyond the binary boundary.\n\nDistributional compression under recursive generation is well-documented \\cite{curious_decline,broken_telephone,markovian_chains}.\nWe introduce \\emph{depth fingerprinting}---classifying text by ordinal generation depth (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+)---and decompose this compression into structurally distinct components: across eight base-model generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B), the NLL distribution's central tendency (mean entropy, lexical diversity) drifts monotonically with depth while its shape (entropy extremes, tail structure) remains an order of magnitude more stable ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$ vs.\\ $d < 0.10$; convergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures confirms this partition).\nWe term this location/shape asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR)---a structural property of fixed-parameter autoregressive sampling that is mechanistically distinct from the tail-coverage erosion in model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion} and analytically informative independent of both the specific mechanism and classification accuracy.\nWhere concurrent work characterizes \\emph{that} and \\emph{why} convergence occurs \\cite{broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, DDR isolates \\emph{what} converges from what is preserved, and leverages the stationary shape component for cross-generator depth prediction without per-generator calibration.\n\nAs a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at 2.4$\\times$ lower inference cost; an end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Clustering-based validation ($k{=}2$, silhouette\\,=\\,0.660) confirms that inference-chain drift separates into a drifting location component ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and a stable shape component ($d < 0.10$). Where prior work documents \\emph{what converges}---diversity loss \\cite{curious_decline}, attractor dynamics \\cite{markovian_chains}---DDR identifies \\emph{what is preserved}: shape invariance provides the cross-generator reference needed for depth detection without per-generator calibration.\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.\n\\end{enumerate}\nA proof-of-concept classifier and systematic boundary mapping across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, and model scale establish where DDR-based detection succeeds and where it fails.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

          " Where concurrent work characterizes \\emph{that} and \\emph{why} convergence occurs \\cite{broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, DDR isolates \\emph{what} converges from what is preserved, and leverages the stationary shape component for cross-generator depth prediction without per-generator calibration.",
          " ",

          "+As a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, surpassing individual detection baselines by 6.7--36.3\\,pp; an end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.",
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "b0716524-3061-4ccf-ba66-603076bc9343",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nAI text detection asks a single binary question---\\emph{human or machine?}---and recent methods answer it with increasing reliability \\cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.\nYet this framing elides a richer structure emerging on the open web: as LLM outputs re-enter training corpora \\cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \\emph{recursive depths}---an estimated 7--17\\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \\cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}---and each generation cycle pushes distributional properties further from both the human source and the first-generation predecessor.\nWhile most deployed LLM outputs use instruction-tuned models, base-model generation pipelines remain prevalent in synthetic data production, pre-training corpus construction, and recursive data augmentation; we accordingly scope this study to base (pretrained) models.\nKnowing \\emph{how many times} text has been recursively generated matters for data provenance \\cite{ccnet,dsir} and model collapse analysis \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}, yet no current method moves beyond the binary boundary.\n\nDistributional compression under recursive generation is well-documented \\cite{curious_decline,broken_telephone,markovian_chains}.\nWe introduce \\emph{depth fingerprinting}---classifying text by ordinal generation depth (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+)---and decompose this compression into structurally distinct components: across eight base-model generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B), the NLL distribution's central tendency (mean entropy, lexical diversity) drifts monotonically with depth while its shape (entropy extremes, tail structure) remains an order of magnitude more stable ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$ vs.\\ $d < 0.10$; convergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures confirms this partition).\nWe term this location/shape asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR)---a structural property of fixed-parameter autoregressive sampling that is mechanistically distinct from the tail-coverage erosion in model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion} and analytically informative independent of both the specific mechanism and classification accuracy.\nWhere concurrent work characterizes \\emph{that} and \\emph{why} convergence occurs \\cite{broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, DDR isolates \\emph{what} converges from what is preserved, and leverages the stationary shape component for cross-generator depth prediction without per-generator calibration.\n\nAs a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, surpassing individual detection baselines by 6.7--36.3\\,pp; an end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Clustering-based validation ($k{=}2$, silhouette\\,=\\,0.660) confirms that inference-chain drift separates into a drifting location component ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and a stable shape component ($d < 0.10$). Where prior work documents \\emph{what converges}---diversity loss \\cite{curious_decline}, attractor dynamics \\cite{markovian_chains}---DDR identifies \\emph{what is preserved}: shape invariance provides the cross-generator reference needed for depth detection without per-generator calibration.\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.\n\\end{enumerate}\nA proof-of-concept classifier and systematic boundary mapping across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, and model scale establish where DDR-based detection succeeds and where it fails.\n",
        "numLines": 22,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 22
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex"
  },
```

```json
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "% Abstract — Depth Fingerprinting of Recursive LLM Generation Chains\n\\begin{abstract}\nWhen base-model LLM outputs re-enter generation pipelines, each recursive step compresses \\emph{where} text sits in distributional space while leaving \\emph{how} it is shaped nearly unchanged.\nWe identify and characterize this asymmetry as \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR) and characterize it across eight \\textbf{base-model} generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B parameters): under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.9$, $\\tau \\leq 0.7$), NLL location statistics and lexical diversity drift monotonically with generation depth (Cohen's $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$), while distributional shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$)---a separation confirmed by convergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures and clustering-based validation (silhouette\\,=\\,0.660).\nWhere prior work on iterative generation documents \\emph{what converges}, the novel observation here is \\emph{what is preserved}: shape invariance under the same process that compresses location provides the stable reference needed for depth estimation without per-generator calibration.\nAs a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble exploiting DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, effective resolution: three depth classes) under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, surpassing individual detection baselines by 6.7--36.3\\,pp; an end-to-end classifier recovers substantially more (F1\\,=\\,0.905, 6-gen LOGO), establishing the analytical framework---not the specific features---as the primary contribution.\nThe signal attenuates under instruction tuning; current applicability is restricted to base models.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 9,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 9
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",
  "uuid": "a7e1647c-d0e1-4cac-b799-ad6398a0bd44",
```

```json
        "content": "\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Comparison with concurrent iterative-generation studies. All three examine how repeated LLM inference transforms text, but differ in chain operator, analytic lens, and downstream goal.}\n\\label{tab:iterative-comparison}\n\\resizebox{\\textwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{2.2cm} p{2.4cm} p{2.4cm} p{2.0cm} c p{3.4cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Study} & \\textbf{Chain Type} & \\textbf{Feature Space} & \\textbf{Analysis Goal} & \\textbf{Generator Scale} & \\textbf{Steps} & \\textbf{Core Insight} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\citet{broken_telephone}\n  & Back-translation (6 bridge langs); rephrasing (ablation)\n  & Surface overlap (BLEU, ROUGE, METEOR, ChrF, BERTScore) + atomic factuality (FActScore)\n  & Factuality degradation rate across iterations\n  & 7--9B (3 open models)\n  & 100\n  & Factual accuracy decays progressively; rate governed by source--bridge linguistic distance \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\citet{markovian_chains}\n  & Rephrasing + round-trip translation\n  & String recurrence time, distinct-output count, similarity plateau (METEOR), KL contraction\n  & Convergence \\& diversity dynamics of inference-time Markov chains\n  & 7--8B (3 open + GPT-4o-mini)\n  & 50\n  & Greedy decoding collapses to 1--4 recurrent outputs; sampling sustains 10--20\\,+ realizations \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\textbf{Ours}\n  & Open-ended continuation (no meaning-preserving constraint)\n  & Token-level NLL statistics (location, scale, shape) + lexical diversity\n  & Depth classification + DDR decomposition into generator \\& depth effects\n  & 0.6--8B (8 models)\n  & 5\n  & NLL location drifts monotonically with depth while shape remains invariant---enabling per-generator depth fingerprinting \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table*}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:iterative-comparison} compares this work with two concurrent studies on iterative LLM generation.\nAll three examine how repeated inference transforms text, but differ in the chain operator (meaning-preserving vs.\\ open-ended), the analytic lens (factuality decay, convergence dynamics, vs.\\ distributional decomposition), and the downstream goal.\nOur continuation-chain setting, which imposes no semantic-preservation constraint, permits richer depth signal and enables the location/shape decomposition that defines DDR.\n",
        "numLines": 41,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 41
      }
    },
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DBbUCMCcAPDxDZarDj2m5e`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Task Formulation}
5	\label{sec:task}
6	
7	Given a text $x$, we predict its \emph{generation depth} $d \in \{0, 1, 2{+}\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).
8	A generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \sim \mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.
9	Depths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
10	
11	\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}
12	\label{sec:generation}
13	
14	We construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \cite{pythia}, \textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \cite{olmo}, \textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \cite{qwen3}, \textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \cite{llama3}, and \textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \cite{mistral7b}.
15	This selection spans parameter scales from 0.6B to 8B and diverse pretraining corpora, enabling evaluation of cross-generator and cross-scale generalization.
16	
17	For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
18	The same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.
19	We use shared seeds across generators so that cross-generator comparisons control for input content and isolate the effect of the generation model from source text variation.
20	In LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
21	We address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
22	Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
23	We generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class analysis shows adjacent depths 2--4 become indistinguishable (F1\,$\approx$\,0.27; Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.
24	All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
25	After filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.
26	
27	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
28	\label{sec:features}
29	
30	The seven features operationalize two complementary views of distributional drift: reference-model features capture how the token-level NLL distribution shifts relative to a fixed model (location and shape statistics), while surface features capture how lexical diversity contracts under recursive generation (type-token statistics).
31	This dual-view design enables the location/shape decomposition that constitutes our primary finding.
32	We deliberately use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent and mechanistically informative; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$11.6\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition.
33	Cross-group ablation (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) and leave-one-feature-out analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}) confirm that both feature groups contribute independently.
34	
35	We extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\ref{tab:features}), selected to provide principled coverage of the NLL distribution---location (mean), spread (std), extremes (max, min), and tail structure (decay)---complemented by two reference-free surface diversity measures.
36	We evaluated six additional NLL statistics (median, 90th percentile, IQR, CV, skewness, kurtosis) as candidate features; all exhibit $|r| > 0.65$ with existing features and yield no classification gain when added ($\Delta$F1\,$\leq$\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\ref{app:feature-candidate}).
37	
38	\begin{table}[t]
39	\centering
40	\footnotesize
41	\begin{tabular}{llp{3.8cm}}
42	\toprule
43	\textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Source} & \textbf{Measures} \\
44	\midrule
45	entropy\_mean & Ref.\ NLL & Central tendency of token surprise \\
46	entropy\_std  & Ref.\ NLL & Spread of token surprise \\
47	entropy\_max  & Ref.\ NLL & Maximum token surprise \\
48	entropy\_min  & Ref.\ NLL & Minimum token surprise \\
49	tail\_decay   & Ref.\ NLL & Log-prob decay slope (ranks 100--1K) \\
50	\midrule
51	TTR           & Surface & Type-token ratio \\
52	hapax\_ratio  & Surface & Proportion of hapax legomena \\
53	\bottomrule
54	\end{tabular}
55	\caption{Seven distributional features. Reference features use Pythia-410M per-token NLL; surface features require no reference model. DDR analysis (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) reveals that the first three drift with depth while the last four remain stable.}
56	\label{tab:features}
57	\end{table}
58	
59	\paragraph{Reference-model features.}
60	A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
61	We use ``entropy'' as shorthand for token-level NLL throughout, following convention in prior detection work; these are point-wise NLL values, not Shannon entropy of the full distribution.
62	Although Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the eight generators, cross-family reference model experiments (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm that depth features are not reference-specific (LOGO F1 varies by $\leq$0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references).
63	From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in the mid-rank region (ranks 100--1000), capturing how steeply probability mass decays beyond high-frequency tokens.
64	
65	\paragraph{Surface features.}
66	Two features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\_ratio).
67	
68	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
69	These features exhibit a \emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).
70	TTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.
71	
72	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
73	\label{sec:evaluation}
74	
75	\paragraph{Cross-validation.}
76	To evaluate generalization, we use \textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation over all eight generators.
77	LOGO holds out individual generators but does not isolate family effects; \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\,=\,0.686; \S\ref{sec:results-classification}).
78	Generator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).
79	
80	\paragraph{Classifier.}
81	We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\ref{tab:baselines}.
82	Alternative classifiers (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}); discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.
83	
84	\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}
85	The primary metric is \textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\% bootstrap CIs ($n{=}1{,}000$).
86	All $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.
87	
88	\paragraph{Baselines.}
89	We compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \cite{ld_score}.
90	Each baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.
91	
92	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
93	Since TTR and hapax\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
```

```json
      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a text $x$, we predict its \\emph{generation depth} $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2{+}\\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).\nA generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \\sim \\mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \\geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.\nDepths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}\n\\label{sec:generation}\n\nWe construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \\textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \\cite{pythia}, \\textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \\cite{olmo}, \\textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \\cite{qwen3}, \\textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \\cite{llama3}, and \\textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \\cite{mistral7b}.\nThis selection spans parameter scales from 0.6B to 8B and diverse pretraining corpora, enabling evaluation of cross-generator and cross-scale generalization.\n\nFor each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\\mathcal{C}_0$.\nThe same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.\nWe use shared seeds across generators so that cross-generator comparisons control for input content and isolate the effect of the generation model from source text variation.\nIn LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.\nWe address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}).\nEach seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\\texttt{Continue the following text: \\{text\\}}''.\nWe generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class analysis shows adjacent depths 2--4 become indistinguishable (F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.27; Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.\nAll generation uses temperature $\\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.\nAfter filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\nThe seven features operationalize two complementary views of distributional drift: reference-model features capture how the token-level NLL distribution shifts relative to a fixed model (location and shape statistics), while surface features capture how lexical diversity contracts under recursive generation (type-token statistics).\nThis dual-view design enables the location/shape decomposition that constitutes our primary finding.\nWe deliberately use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent and mechanistically informative; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$11.6\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition.\nCross-group ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) and leave-one-feature-out analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}) confirm that both feature groups contribute independently.\n\nWe extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\\ref{tab:features}), selected to provide principled coverage of the NLL distribution---location (mean), spread (std), extremes (max, min), and tail structure (decay)---complemented by two reference-free surface diversity measures.\nWe evaluated six additional NLL statistics (median, 90th percentile, IQR, CV, skewness, kurtosis) as candidate features; all exhibit $|r| > 0.65$ with existing features and yield no classification gain when added ($\\Delta$F1\\,$\\leq$\\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-candidate}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{llp{3.8cm}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature} & \\textbf{Source} & \\textbf{Measures} \\\\\n\\midrule\nentropy\\_mean & Ref.\\ NLL & Central tendency of token surprise \\\\\nentropy\\_std  & Ref.\\ NLL & Spread of token surprise \\\\\nentropy\\_max  & Ref.\\ NLL & Maximum token surprise \\\\\nentropy\\_min  & Ref.\\ NLL & Minimum token surprise \\\\\ntail\\_decay   & Ref.\\ NLL & Log-prob decay slope (ranks 100--1K) \\\\\n\\midrule\nTTR           & Surface & Type-token ratio \\\\\nhapax\\_ratio  & Surface & Proportion of hapax legomena \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Seven distributional features. Reference features use Pythia-410M per-token NLL; surface features require no reference model. DDR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) reveals that the first three drift with depth while the last four remain stable.}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-model features.}\nA held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nWe use ``entropy'' as shorthand for token-level NLL throughout, following convention in prior detection work; these are point-wise NLL values, not Shannon entropy of the full distribution.\nAlthough Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the eight generators, cross-family reference model experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm that depth features are not reference-specific (LOGO F1 varies by $\\leq$0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references).\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in the mid-rank region (ranks 100--1000), capturing how steeply probability mass decays beyond high-frequency tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\\_ratio).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThese features exhibit a \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).\nTTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-validation.}\nTo evaluate generalization, we use \\textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation over all eight generators.\nLOGO holds out individual generators but does not isolate family effects; \\textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\nGenerator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier.}\nWe use XGBoost \\cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}.\nAlternative classifiers (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \\cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}); discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($n{=}1{,}000$).\nAll $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n",
```

```text
1	To verify that the seven retained features are not an arbitrary subset, we screened six additional NLL-distributional statistics as candidate features: median, 90th percentile (p90), interquartile range (IQR), coefficient of variation (CV), skewness, and kurtosis.
2	Table~\ref{tab:candidate-features} reports their DDR coordinates, classification impact, and correlation with retained features.
3	
4	\begin{table}[ht]
5	\centering
6	\small
7	\caption{Candidate feature screening. All six candidates fall in the drifting DDR zone and correlate strongly ($|r| > 0.65$) with existing features. Adding any combination to the baseline degrades or leaves unchanged LOGO 3-class F1; LOFO $\Delta > 0$ indicates that removing the candidate \emph{improves} classification.}
8	\label{tab:candidate-features}
9	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
10	\begin{tabular}{lccccl}
11	\toprule
12	\textbf{Candidate} & $|\rho|$ & $d$ & \textbf{LOFO} $\Delta$\textbf{F1} & $|r|_{\max}$ & \textbf{Most correlated} \\
13	\midrule
14	median    & 0.685 & 0.794 & $+$0.008 & 0.960 & entropy\_mean \\
15	p90       & 0.693 & 0.960 & $+$0.000 & 0.937 & entropy\_mean \\
16	IQR       & 0.708 & 1.079 & $+$0.007 & 0.908 & entropy\_std \\
17	CV        & 0.633 & 0.980 & $-$0.000 & 0.761 & entropy\_mean \\
18	skewness  & 0.651 & 0.940 & $+$0.010 & 0.780 & entropy\_mean \\
19	kurtosis  & 0.635 & 0.769 & $-$0.003 & 0.650 & entropy\_mean \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	All six candidates occupy the drifting zone ($|\rho| > 0.63$, $d > 0.77$), overlapping with entropy mean, TTR, and hapax ratio; none introduces a new axis of depth-dependent variation.
25	Three candidates---median ($r{=}0.96$), p90 ($r{=}0.94$), and IQR ($r{=}0.91$)---are near-perfect linear proxies of existing features.
26	Adding all six to the 6-feature baseline\footnote{Tail decay requires full-vocabulary logits unavailable in the token-NLL subset used for this analysis; we report 6-feature baselines for fair comparison.} reduces 3-class LOGO F1 from 0.733 to 0.722, consistent with overfitting from redundant inputs.
27	LOFO analysis on the extended 12-feature set confirms that removing skewness ($+$0.010), median ($+$0.008), or IQR ($+$0.007) \emph{improves} classification, indicating these features inject noise rather than signal.
28	The retained seven features span three complementary DDR zones (drifting, intermediate, near-stationary) and two modalities (reference-model NLL, surface lexical diversity); additional distributional statistics provide no incremental information.
29	
```

```text
1	\subsection*{Data-Driven Clustering}
2	
3	To assess the DDR partition structure and determine whether the drifting/non-drifting split is data-driven, we apply $k$-means clustering ($k \in \{2,3,4,5\}$) to the seven features in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space (Table~\ref{tab:clustering}).
4	
5	\begin{table}[ht]
6	\centering
7	\small
8	\caption{$k$-means clustering of 7 features in $(|\rho|, d)$ space.}
9	\label{tab:clustering}
10	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
11	\begin{tabular}{ccp{5.5cm}}
12	\toprule
13	$k$ & Silhouette & Clusters \\
14	\midrule
15	2 & \textbf{0.660} & \{$\bar{H}$, TTR, hapax\} $\mid$ \{$\sigma_H$, $H_{\min}$, $H_{\max}$, tail\} \\
16	3 & 0.532 & \{$\bar{H}$, TTR, hapax\} $\mid$ \{$\sigma_H$, $H_{\max}$, tail\} $\mid$ \{$H_{\min}$\} \\
17	4 & 0.527 & \{$\bar{H}$, TTR, hapax\} $\mid$ \{$H_{\max}$, tail\} $\mid$ \{$\sigma_H$\} $\mid$ \{$H_{\min}$\} \\
18	\bottomrule
19	\end{tabular}}
20	\end{table}
21	
22	The primary drifting/non-drifting split ($k{=}2$, silhouette\,=\,0.660) is the strongest cluster structure.
23	At $k{=}3$, entropy min forms its own cluster rather than joining the near-stationary group because its pooled $|\rho|{=}0.40$ (driven by the gen-0/gen-1 boundary) places it far from other near-stationary features in $\rho$ space.
24	The 3/1/3 DDR partition resolves this by using the gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ Cohen's $d$ ($d{=}0.03$) to place entropy min in the near-stationary group, a domain-informed refinement that unsupervised clustering cannot recover from pooled statistics alone.
25	At $k{=}4$ (silhouette\,=\,0.571), entropy std separates from the near-stationary group, consistent with its intermediate DDR classification.
26	
27	\paragraph{Exhaustive partition enumeration.}
28	To confirm that the three-vs-four split is optimal rather than incidental, we enumerate all $\binom{7}{3}{=}35$ possible ways to assign three features to a ``drifting'' group and the remaining four to ``non-drifting,'' computing the silhouette score for each partition in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space.
29	The observed DDR partition (\{entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio\}) achieves the highest silhouette (0.660), with a gap of 0.18 to the second-ranked split; 20 of 35 partitions produce negative silhouette scores.
30	
31	\subsection*{TOST Equivalence Bound Sensitivity}
32	\label{app:tost-sensitivity}
33	
34	Table~\ref{tab:tost-sensitivity} shows the fraction of generators for which each feature passes the TOST equivalence test (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, $\alpha{=}0.05$) at three equivalence bounds $\varepsilon$.
35	
36	\begin{table}[ht]
37	\centering
38	\small
39	\caption{TOST equivalence pass rate across 8 generators at varying $\varepsilon$.}
40	\label{tab:tost-sensitivity}
41	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
42	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
43	\toprule
44	\textbf{DDR Zone} & \textbf{Feature} & $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon{=}0.15}$ & $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon{=}0.20}$ & $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon{=}0.25}$ \\
45	\midrule
46	\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & $\bar{H}$ (ent.\ mean) & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\
47	 & TTR & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\
48	 & hapax ratio & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\
49	\midrule
50	Intermediate & $\sigma_H$ (ent.\ std) & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\
51	\midrule
52	\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & $H_{\min}$ (ent.\ min) & 7/8 & 8/8 & 8/8 \\
53	 & $H_{\max}$ (ent.\ max) & 6/8 & 6/8 & 6/8 \\
54	 & tail decay & 4/8 & 4/8 & 7/8 \\
55	\bottomrule
56	\end{tabular}}
57	\end{table}
58	
59	The partition is robust to the equivalence bound: drifting features reject equivalence at all $\varepsilon$ for all generators, while entropy min passes even at the stricter bound $\varepsilon{=}0.15$ (7/8 generators).
60	Tail decay is the most $\varepsilon$-sensitive near-stationary feature, passing 4/8 at $\varepsilon{=}0.20$ but 7/8 at $\varepsilon{=}0.25$; the four non-passing generators show $|d|{=}0.15$--$0.22$, still well below the drifting threshold ($d > 0.50$).
61	Entropy max is stable across all three bounds (6/8), with the two failures (Pythia-1B $d{=}0.21$, Pythia-2.8B $d{=}0.25$) reflecting same-family reference affinity with the Pythia-410M reference model.
62	
63	\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.}
64	To address concerns about the sensitivity of the drifting-boundary threshold,
65	we sweep $|d|$ from $0.10$ to $0.80$ in increments of $0.05$ and record the
66	resulting binary partition (drifting vs.\ stationary) along with the silhouette
67	score in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space.
68	Across the entire range, only \emph{three} distinct partitions emerge,
69	reflecting natural gaps between feature clusters rather than threshold artifacts.
70	The three-feature drifting set (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio)
71	is stable across $|d| \in [0.50,\, 0.75]$ with a silhouette of $0.660$---the
72	peak value in the sweep.
73	Including the intermediate feature entropy std ($d{=}0.49$) yields a
74	4/3 partition stable across $|d| \in [0.10,\, 0.45]$ (silhouette\,=\,0.451).
75	The partition used in our analysis corresponds to the peak-silhouette region,
76	confirming that the drifting/stationary boundary reflects a natural gap in the
77	$(|\rho|, d)$ landscape rather than an artifact of a particular
78	threshold choice.
79	
80	\paragraph{Partition stability across $\varepsilon$ thresholds.}
81	\label{app:epsilon-sweep}
82	
83	The DDR partition classifies features as non-stationary ($d \geq \varepsilon$) or stationary ($d < \varepsilon$) based on pooled Cohen's $d$ (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
84	Table~\ref{tab:epsilon-sweep} reports the critical $\varepsilon$ at which each feature's classification changes; the three drifting and three near-stationary features maintain their classification for all $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$.
85	
86	\begin{table}[ht]
87	\centering
88	\small
89	\caption{Partition stability under varying $\varepsilon$. Critical threshold: the $\varepsilon$ above which a feature is reclassified as stationary. The drifting/near-stationary split is invariant for $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$; only entropy std ($d{=}0.55$) changes at $\varepsilon > 0.55$.}
90	\label{tab:epsilon-sweep}
91	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
92	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
93	\toprule
94	\textbf{DDR Zone} & \textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Pooled $d$} & \textbf{Stationary at $\varepsilon >$} \\
95	\midrule
96	\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & $\bar{H}$ (ent.\ mean) & 1.17 & 1.17 \\
97	 & TTR & 0.88 & 0.88 \\
98	 & hapax ratio & 0.76 & 0.76 \\
99	\midrule
100	Intermediate & $\sigma_H$ (ent.\ std) & 0.55 & 0.55 \\
101	\midrule
102	\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & $H_{\max}$ (ent.\ max) & 0.08 & 0.08 \\
103	 & tail decay & 0.08 & 0.08 \\
104	 & $H_{\min}$ (ent.\ min) & 0.016 & 0.016 \\
105	\bottomrule
106	\end{tabular}}
107	\end{table}
108	
109	\noindent The 3/1/3 partition is stable across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$: the three drifting features remain non-stationary at all $\varepsilon < 0.76$, the three near-stationary features remain stationary at all $\varepsilon > 0.08$, and only entropy std changes classification (becoming stationary at $\varepsilon > 0.55$), confirming it as the sole threshold-sensitive feature.
110	The minimum gap between adjacent zone boundaries ($d_{\text{hapax}} - d_{\sigma_H} = 0.21$) exceeds typical effect-size estimation uncertainty, providing a margin against sampling noise.
111	
112	\subsection*{Holdout Generator Split Validation}
113	\label{app:holdout-split}
114	
115	To confirm the DDR partition is not an artifact of the specific generator pool, we split the eight generators into disjoint selection (4) and validation (4) sets, compute per-subset pooled $(|\rho|, d)$, and check whether all seven features receive the same zone assignment in both subsets.
116	We evaluate two complementary splits to avoid dependence on a single partition:
117	Split~A selects \{Pythia-2.8B, Qwen3-1.7B, OLMo, Mistral\} (validation: Pythia-1B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B, Llama);
118	Split~B selects \{Pythia-1B, Qwen3-0.6B, OLMo, Mistral\} (validation: Pythia-2.8B, Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-8B, Llama).
119	
120	\begin{table}[ht]
121	\centering
122	\small
123	\caption{Holdout split validation of the DDR partition. Selection (sel) and validation (val) $|\rho|$ and $d$ for two disjoint 4/4 generator splits. Zone assignment is identical across all four subsets for every feature.}
124	\label{tab:holdout-split}
125	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
126	\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}
127	\toprule
128	& & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{Split A}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{Split B}} \\
129	\cmidrule(lr){3-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-8}
130	\textbf{Zone} & \textbf{Feature} & sel $|\rho|$ & val $|\rho|$ & $\Delta|\rho|$ & sel $|\rho|$ & val $|\rho|$ & $\Delta|\rho|$ \\
131	\midrule
132	\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & $\bar{H}$ (ent.\ mean) & 0.60 & 0.58 & 0.02 & 0.60 & 0.58 & 0.02 \\
133	 & TTR & 0.61 & 0.59 & 0.02 & 0.60 & 0.59 & 0.01 \\
134	 & hapax ratio & 0.62 & 0.60 & 0.02 & 0.62 & 0.61 & 0.01 \\
135	\midrule
136	Intermediate & $\sigma_H$ (ent.\ std) & 0.19 & 0.21 & 0.02 & 0.22 & 0.17 & 0.05 \\
137	\midrule
138	\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & $H_{\min}$ (ent.\ min) & 0.39 & 0.36 & 0.03 & 0.38 & 0.37 & 0.01 \\
139	 & $H_{\max}$ (ent.\ max) & 0.05 & 0.05 & 0.00 & 0.04 & 0.06 & 0.02 \\
140	 & tail decay & 0.10 & 0.08 & 0.02 & 0.11 & 0.07 & 0.04 \\
141	\bottomrule
142	\end{tabular}}
143	\end{table}
144	
145	Under simple joint $(|\rho|, d)$ thresholds, both splits produce a 3/2/2 partition: entropy min is classified as a second intermediate feature due to its elevated pooled $|\rho|$ (0.36--0.39).
146	However, entropy min's binary (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) $d$ values are uniformly below 0.06 across all four split${\times}$subset combinations, consistent with near-stationarity on the task-relevant metric; its pooled $|\rho|$ reflects change concentrated at the gen-0/gen-1 boundary rather than sustained drift.
147	Five of seven features are unambiguously classified under any reasonable criterion; entropy min's boundary status is resolved by the binary-task $d$ criterion.
148	
149	Selection and validation subsets produce identical partitions in both splits (Table~\ref{tab:holdout-split}; Figure~\ref{fig:holdout-scatter}).
150	The maximum $\Delta|\rho|$ between selection and validation is 0.05 (entropy std, Split~B), and the minimum gap between adjacent zones remains $>$0.15 in all subsets.
151	
152	\begin{figure}[H]
153	\centering
154	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig_ddr_holdout_split.pdf}
155	\caption{Holdout split validation in $(|\rho|, d)$ space. Filled markers: selection set; hollow: validation set. Zone assignments are identical across subsets; selection/validation pairs cluster tightly for all features.}
156	\label{fig:holdout-scatter}
157	\end{figure}
158	
159	\subsection*{Heteroscedasticity Sensitivity}
160	\label{app:heteroscedasticity}
161	
162	Levene tests reject equal variance between gen-1 and gen-2+ for drifting features ($p < 10^{-10}$; variance ratios 1.86--2.21).
163	To verify that the DDR partition is robust to this heteroscedasticity, we supplement pooled Cohen's $d$ with Glass's $\Delta$ (using the gen-1 standard deviation as denominator) and Cliff's $\delta$ (a non-parametric ordinal effect size; Table~\ref{tab:effect-sizes}).
164	
165	\begin{table}[ht]
166	\centering
167	\small
168	\caption{Effect size comparison (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, $N$\,=\,29{,}095). Glass's $\Delta$ uses the gen-1 SD as denominator; Cliff's $\delta$ thresholds follow Romano et al.\ (2006): $|\delta| < 0.147$ negligible, $< 0.33$ small, $< 0.474$ medium, otherwise large.}
169	\label{tab:effect-sizes}
170	\begin{tabular}{llrrr}
171	\toprule
172	\textbf{Zone} & \textbf{Feature} & \textbf{$|d|$} & \textbf{$|\Delta|$} & \textbf{$|\delta|$} \\
173	\midrule
174	\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & ent.\ mean & 1.054 & 1.410 & 0.587 \\
175	 & TTR & 0.814 & 1.116 & 0.510 \\
176	 & hapax ratio & 0.735 & 0.936 & 0.528 \\
177	\midrule
178	Intermediate & ent.\ std & 0.457 & 0.605 & 0.295 \\
179	\midrule
180	\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & tail decay & 0.087 & 0.099 & 0.072 \\
181	 & ent.\ max & 0.062 & 0.065 & 0.021 \\
182	 & ent.\ min & 0.016 & 0.015 & 0.354$^{\dagger}$ \\
183	\bottomrule
184	\end{tabular}
185	\end{table}
186	
187	\noindent Effect sizes in Table~\ref{tab:effect-sizes} are computed on the binary task (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+); the main text (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) reports 3-class pooled $d$ values (e.g., hapax ratio $d{=}0.76$), which are slightly larger because the gen-0/gen-1 contrast contributes additional separation.
188	Glass's $\Delta$ amplifies drifting effects (1.41 vs.\ 1.05 for entropy mean) because gen-2+ variance exceeds gen-1 variance (ratio 2.08), making the pooled Cohen's $d$ conservative.
189	Near-stationary features remain negligible under both parametric metrics ($|\Delta| \leq 0.099$).
190	The minimum drifting-to-maximum-near-stationary gap is $9.5\times$ under Glass's $\Delta$ (0.936\,/\,0.099); heteroscedasticity does not erode the partition.
191	Cliff's $\delta$ classifies all drifting features as ``large'' ($|\delta| > 0.474$) and entropy std as ``small'' ($|\delta|{=}0.295$), consistent with the parametric partition.
192	
193	\noindent $^{\dagger}$Entropy min's elevated Cliff's $\delta$ (0.354, ``medium'') reflects distributional shape asymmetry between gen-1 and gen-2+ that rank-based statistics detect but location measures do not (Cohen's $d{=}0.016$, Glass's $\Delta{=}0.015$).
194	This is consistent with its documented boundary status (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}): the gen-0/gen-1 transition produces rank-order shifts that persist into deeper generations without affecting the mean.
195
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	Recursive generation alters \emph{where} the token-level surprise distribution sits but not \emph{how} it is shaped---a dichotomy with a mechanistic basis.
5	Under nucleus sampling, top-$p$ truncation preserves the functional form of the conditional distribution at each step; what changes is the \emph{input}: as the model conditions on increasingly self-typical outputs, expected NLL compresses monotonically.
6	This yields two testable predictions: (1)~increasing sampling randomness should weaken the location drift, and (2)~semantic-preservation constraints should attenuate it.
7	Both are confirmed: at $t{=}1.0$, location signal collapses ($\rho$: $-0.60 \to -0.002$; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); under elaboration prompts, drift weakens (F1\,=\,0.730 vs.\ 0.781; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
8	Causal analyses corroborate: drifting features contribute $5.5\times$ more to classification, with 6/7 exhibiting significant Granger causality over the five-point chain (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-causality}).
9	Intuitively, top-$p$ truncation restricts token selection but preserves the conditional distribution's functional form; shape statistics are thus inherited from pretrained weights, while entropy std ($d{=}0.55$) shows intermediate behavior as a second-order location effect ($\text{Var} = E[X^2] - E[X]^2$ responds to mean shift even when shape is preserved).
10	These results are established under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$); at $\tau{=}1.0$---where truncation is near-minimal---shape features remain stable ($d < 0.15$; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}), but verification under non-autoregressive decoding strategies remains open.
11	
12	The classifier is a proof-of-concept for corpus-level auditing, not per-document filtering: gen-1 per-class F1\,=\,0.63 is insufficient for individual flagging but supports batch-level contamination estimation (${\pm}3$--$4$\% at $n{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}).
13	Depth-aware filtering can serve as a prophylactic against model collapse \cite{drayson_detection_prevents_collapse}, though depth-stratified curation does not yet improve downstream training (TOST equivalence at $\varepsilon{=}{\pm}2\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:downstream})---consistent with depth compressing surface statistics rather than the semantic content driving task performance; the primary value lies in provenance, not training improvement.
14	
15	DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage by updating weights, whereas inference-chain drift at fixed parameters shifts only location, leaving shape stable.
16	Vocabulary impoverishment alone cannot explain this asymmetry: if depth simplified the lexicon, shape features should also compress, yet they remain stable ($d < 0.10$; \S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
17	Prior work documents \emph{what converges}---diversity loss \cite{curious_decline}, attractor dynamics \cite{markovian_chains}---but not what is \emph{preserved}; this complementary observation enables depth detection without per-generator calibration.
18	The decomposition itself---identifying which distributional properties carry cross-generator depth signal---constitutes the analytical contribution, independent of whether shape stability originates from sampling truncation or deeper autoregressive properties.
19	Three depth classes represent the practical resolution ceiling (5-class F1\,$\approx$\,0.50; Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}); QLoRA recovers more signal (F1\,=\,0.905, 6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), reflecting token-order patterns that aggregate statistics miss.
20	Instruction-tuned models substantially compress DDR structure (silhouette 0.660$\to$0.372; entropy-mean $\rho$ reverses sign; 3-class F1 degrades 3.7--19.9\,pp), with model-dependent heterogeneity ruling out a uniform mechanism (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}).
21	Yet QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 on instruct data, confirming that depth signal persists at the token-sequence level beyond DDR's summary-statistic decomposition.
22	Cross-domain evaluation (News, Academic, Code; $t{=}1.0$) yields binary F1\,=\,0.74--0.75 but degraded 3-class F1 (0.62--0.65; Appendix~\ref{app:domain}), with temperature and domain effects confounded.
23	Our generation chains use open-ended continuation prompts; real-world recursive pipelines---instruction-following chains, multi-turn dialogues, mixed-model editing---may attenuate, restructure, or introduce confounds not present in controlled chains.
24	
```

</details>

what changes is the \\emph{input}: as the model conditions on increasingly self-typical outputs, expected NLL compresses monotonically.\nThis yields two testable predictions: (1)~increasing sampling randomness should weaken the location drift, and (2)~semantic-preservation constraints should attenuate it.\nBoth are confirmed: at $t{=}1.0$, location signal collapses ($\\rho$: $-0.60 \\to -0.002$; <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
Let me check the abstract again. From the earlier read:

```
% Abstract — Depth Fingerprinting of Recursive LLM Generation Chains
\begin{abstract}
When base-model LLM outputs re-enter generation pipelines, each recursive step compresses \emph{where} text sits in distributional space while leaving \emph{how} it is shaped nearly unchanged.
We identify and characterize this asymmetry as \emph{differential depth response} (DDR) and characterize it across eight \textbf{base-model} generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B parameters): under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.9$, $\tau \leq 0.7$), NLL location statistics and lexical diversity drift monotonically with generation depth (Cohen's $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$), while distributional shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$)---a separation confirmed by convergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-
```

The key insight is that while location shifts predictably across depths, shape stays stable—which means I can use shape as an anchor for depth detection without needing to retrain for each model. The practical validation shows a seven-feature ensemble built on this principle hits 0.694 F1 on held-out generators, beating individual baselines by a significant margin, and it handles three depth classes effectively.

But the full end-to-end classifier does much better at 0.905 F1 across six generators, which tells me the framework itself is what matters, not the specific features I chose. The effect weakens once instruction tuning enters the picture, so this approach works best with base models as they come.

```

Let me verify the checklist:
1. ✅ "identify and characterize" phrasing is correct
2. ✅ "base-model" is bolded
3. ✅ The leak-free binary F1 score with the three-class resolution is in there
4. ✅ The ensemble outperforms individual baselines by the right margin
5. ✅ Convergence across effect-size measures is mentioned
6. ✅ Instruction tuning signal attenuation and base-model restriction are both there
7. ✅ Clustering validation with silhouette score is included

I notice "characterize" does repeat in one spot—"identify and characterize this asymmetry as DDR and characterize it across"—but that's minor. The QLoRA result definitely has the six-generator LOGO qualifier, so that's solid. The abstract is coming together well.

Now looking at what still needs attention: the ensemble claim in the abstract mentions surpassing individual baselines by 6.7–36.3 percentage points, which is accurate, though it doesn't explicitly note that the four-detector ensemble reaches comparable performance to the best single detector. That's a fair observation but the Results section handles it honestly. The title "Depth Fingerprinting" with the subtitle "Classifying Recursive Generation Depth" seems defensible—R2 supports keeping it despite the three-class limitation. And I should check the page count issue flagged in R34-V1.

For the remaining work, I'm prioritizing quick wins: removing the redundant "characterize" in the abstract, adding those three missing citations (Tulchinskii, Briesch, Diaz & Karampatziakis) to related work and the bibliography, and improving the Figure 3 caption. These are straightforward edits that show attention to detail without risking layout changes this close to the deadline.

The Agent already has a full queue, so I'm holding off on the medium-impact batch—the Granger analysis caveat, terminology consistency between DDR pattern and partition, and color alignment across figures can wait. Right now I need to clean up the PDF images and let the GPU experiment finish running, then check back once the queue clears.
````

```json
    "originalFile": "% Abstract — Depth Fingerprinting of Recursive LLM Generation Chains\n\\begin{abstract}\nWhen base-model LLM outputs re-enter generation pipelines, each recursive step compresses \\emph{where} text sits in distributional space while leaving \\emph{how} it is shaped nearly unchanged.\nWe identify and characterize this asymmetry as \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR) and characterize it across eight \\textbf{base-model} generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B parameters): under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.9$, $\\tau \\leq 0.7$), NLL location statistics and lexical diversity drift monotonically with generation depth (Cohen's $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$), while distributional shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$)---a separation confirmed by convergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures and clustering-based validation (silhouette\\,=\\,0.660).\nWhere prior work on iterative generation documents \\emph{what converges}, the novel observation here is \\emph{what is preserved}: shape invariance under the same process that compresses location provides the stable reference needed for depth estimation without per-generator calibration.\nAs a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble exploiting DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, effective resolution: three depth classes) under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, surpassing individual detection baselines by 6.7--36.3\\,pp; an end-to-end classifier recovers substantially more (F1\\,=\\,0.905, 6-gen LOGO), establishing the analytical framework---not the specific features---as the primary contribution.\nThe signal attenuates under instruction tuning; current applicability is restricted to base models.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Recursive generation in base models leaves a systematic distributional signature: central-tendency features drift monotonically with depth while shape features remain an order of magnitude more stable---a dichotomy we characterize as differential depth response (DDR), validated across eight base-model generators from five families.
5	This decomposition enables cross-generator depth classification (leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694; end-to-end: 0.905 under 6-gen LOGO) and provides an analytical lens complementary to model collapse: fixed-parameter sampling compresses distributional location while preserving shape, whereas retraining erodes both.
6	Current boundaries are instruction tuning---where alignment compresses DDR structure (silhouette 0.660$\to$0.372) while token-level signal persists (QLoRA 0.89--0.91)---and frontier scale, where preliminary 14B evidence suggests the partition persists with attenuated effect sizes.
7	
```

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
5	\label{sec:results-classification}
6	
7	The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.762 (95\% CI [0.758, 0.766]; $N$\,=\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}), with cross-generator F1 spread of 9.1\,pp capturing generator selection uncertainty.
8	Because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}), gen-0 classification is near-perfect (per-class F1\,$\approx$\,0.94), inflating the 3-class metric.
9	We therefore adopt \textbf{leak-free binary F1} (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+)\,=\,0.694 (CI [0.688, 0.700]) as the primary metric throughout: it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing.
10	Per-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B), with the primary difficulty concentrated at the gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary (Appendix~\ref{app:confusion}).
11	
12	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
13	Cross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield binary F1\,=\,0.830 (95\% CI [.818, .841]) under 5-generator LOGO.\footnote{5-generator subset (Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B); generator-level LOGO with bootstrap CIs ($n$\,=\,2{,}000). Not directly comparable to 8-generator LOGO due to differing subsets.}
14	Single-generator transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}) confirms the signal is not a mixed-chain artifact: classifiers trained on one generator transfer to all others with mean binary F1\,=\,0.810; LOFO yields 0.686.
15	A scale effect emerges: Pythia achieves the highest F1 (0.718--0.724) while larger models degrade (Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (cross-reference spread $\leq$0.013; \S\ref{sec:results-differential}); cross-domain signal persists with variation (Appendix~\ref{app:domain}).
16	An end-to-end QLoRA classifier achieves 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 under 6-generator LOGO ($+$11.6\,pp vs.\ the same-subset XGBoost baseline of 0.789; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that richer token-level representations capture additional depth signal; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 within-model (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the empirical decomposition---not the specific classifier---as DDR's primary contribution.
17	
18	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
19	Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\,=\,0.724 (vs.\ 0.770 unmatched); a random-subsample control confirms the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
20	
21	\paragraph{Seed-sharing control.}
22	Seed-level demeaning (F1\,=\,0.701) and an independent-seed experiment with disjoint seed pools (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}) both confirm the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.
23

26	\footnotesize
27	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%

32	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 2.8B & \textbf{0.724} & [.707, .742] & 0.798 \\
33	Pythia-1B       & Pythia  & 1.0B & 0.718 & [.700, .735] & \textbf{0.806} \\

37	Qwen3-8B       & Qwen    & 8.2B & 0.692 & [.674, .708] & 0.714 \\
```
