Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is the primary metric; it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing. Cross-generator binary F1: SD\,=\,0.028, IQR\,=\,[0.692, 0.714]. $^{\dagger}$3-class F1 carries upward bias because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}); independent-seed controls confirm the depth signal (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}).}
```text
46	\label{tab:logo-results}
47	\end{table}
48	
49	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
50	\label{sec:results-differential}
51	
52	\begin{figure*}[t]
53	\centering
54	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}
55	\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth. \textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\rho| > 0.59$). \textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (blue for drifting, amber for stationary). This dichotomy defines the differential depth response (DDR).}
56	\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}
57	\end{figure*}
58	
59	Not all distributional properties respond equally to recursive generation.
60	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals a striking dichotomy that holds across all eight generators: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat.
61	We term this asymmetry the \emph{differential depth response} (DDR).
62	The primary partition is binary: $k{=}2$ clustering in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space yields silhouette\,=\,0.660 (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}), cleanly separating three \emph{drifting} features ($|\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) from four \emph{non-drifting} ones ($d \leq 0.55$); higher $k$ degrades cluster quality ($k{=}3$: silhouette\,=\,0.532).
63	A domain-informed refinement resolves entropy std as a sole \emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; CV shift $<$3\% vs.\ $>$15\% for drifting features).
64	Convergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures converge on this partition, confirming that the location/shape asymmetry reflects distributional structure rather than feature-selection artifact (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}, Quantification).
65	
66	\begin{figure}[t]
67	\centering
68	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
69	\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values. Drifting features (blue; $|\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) show strong monotonic depth sensitivity; non-drifting features remain stable. Entropy std (wine; $d{=}0.55$) is an intermediate member; non-drifting features (amber; $d < 0.10$) show negligible drift. Entropy min ($|\rho|{=}0.40$ pooled; gen-0/gen-1 sensitive) and tail decay (TOST 4/8) are boundary features within this group.}
70	\label{fig:feature-drift}
71	\end{figure}
72	
73	Pooled class-balanced Spearman correlations with depth confirm the binary split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
74	drifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.64$, TTR $\rho = -0.63$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.60$),
75	while non-drifting features remain near-zero (tail decay $|\rho| = 0.04$; per-depth distributions in Appendix~\ref{app:feature-distributions}).
76	The six-fold magnitude gap between drifting ($|\rho| > 0.60$) and non-drifting features ($|\rho| \leq 0.16$) defines the DDR partition.
77	Within the non-drifting group, entropy std ($|\rho| = 0.16$, $d{=}0.55$) shows a substantial level shift despite low monotonicity, distinguishing it from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$); this refinement is stable across $d$ thresholds in $[0.56, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
78	
79	\paragraph{Quantification.}
80	Convergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures confirm the split.
81	Cohen's $d$ \cite{cohen1988statistical} shows a ${\geq}7\times$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and near-stationary features ($d \leq 0.08$); KS statistics, Glass's $\Delta$, Cliff's $\delta$, and CV shift all corroborate (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
82	Four features fall cleanly into drifting or non-drifting groups.
83	Entropy min and tail decay are \emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1 ($|\rho|{=}0.40$); tail decay passes TOST for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).
84	Entropy std ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$) is the sole intermediate member.
85	\paragraph{Validation.}
86	The drifting/non-drifting boundary is not manually set: it emerges from $k{=}2$ clustering in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space and is invariant across threshold choices $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
87	Per-generator TOST equivalence testing ($\varepsilon{=}|d|{<}0.20$, following Cohen's~\citeyearpar{cohen1988statistical} conventional threshold; gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8 pass) and entropy max (6/8); tail decay passes for only 4/8, with $|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$ spanning the boundary.
88	A permutation test ($n{=}10{,}000$) confirms the three-vs-four split is non-random ($p{=}0.030$; fixed-label silhouette\,=\,0.660 vs.\ null mean\,=\,0.46); exhaustive enumeration of all $\binom{7}{3}{=}35$ possible splits ranks this partition first (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
89	Generator-split consistency further validates the partition: the drifting/non-drifting boundary persists across all eight hold-out generators.
90	Reclassifying either boundary feature as intermediate preserves the core drifting/non-drifting split and leaves classification unchanged.
91	
92	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
93	The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.773) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table}).
94	PCA confirms the three drifting features share a single latent dimension (92.3\% variance; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}); replacing them with PC1 costs only 1.1\,pp, confirming that DDR's contribution is identifying \emph{which} distributional properties drift, not classification per se.
95	TTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are both retained for marginal LOFO gains despite feature masking \cite{strobl_conditional_2008} (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
96	
97	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
98	Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable LOGO F1 (spread 0.013; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}), bounding the same-family affinity effect; depth features are robust to reference model choice, though residual same-family proximity may inflate Pythia-family F1 by up to 1.3\,pp.
99	
100	\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}
101	All four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-9.3$ to $-26.0$\,pp binary F1); low-temperature improvement is family-dependent ($+7$--$9$\,pp for Pythia/OLMo at $t{=}0.5$, negligible for Qwen; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}).
102	Continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\,=\,0.781), with cross-prompt-type transfer confirming shared signal (LOPTO F1\,=\,0.753; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}); the DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures despite absolute effect-size compression (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).
103	
104	
105	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
106	\label{sec:results-baselines}
107	
108	Existing detection methods target binary real-vs-synthetic classification; we test whether their continuous scores incidentally encode recursive depth by repurposing four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
109	
110	\begin{table}[t]
111	\centering
112	\footnotesize
113	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
114	\toprule
115	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{3-cl F1} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{ms/text} \\
116	\midrule
117	4-det.\ + surface     & 6 & \textbf{0.795} & \textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\
118	4-detector ens.\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\
119	7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \textbf{9.7} \\
120	\midrule
121	Avg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\
122	FastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\
123	LD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\
124	Binoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\
125	\bottomrule
126	\end{tabular}
127	\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled evaluation; slightly higher than per-generator macro average in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}). Identical XGBoost configuration. 7-feature: one 410M model (905\,MB); 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B params (2{,}868\,MB). Individual detection scores (N/A) share the reference-model forward pass; per-method timing is not separately profiled.}
128	\label{tab:baselines}
129	\end{table}
130	
131	\begin{figure}[t]
132	\centering
133	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}
134	\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\ inference cost in ms/text (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M reference model) matches multi-detector ensembles (two models, 1.4B total) at 2.4$\times$ lower cost; individual detectors vary widely when repurposed for depth.}
135	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
136	\end{figure}
137	
138	The 7-feature ensemble surpasses all individual baselines in 3-class F1 ($\Delta$\,=\,6.7--36.3\,pp; Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) and the pure 4-detector ensemble in binary F1 (0.705 vs.\ 0.700).
139	Adding DDR's surface features to the four detectors yields the highest binary F1 (0.708), marginally above the 7-feature set at 2.4$\times$ the inference cost.
140	This result underscores that DDR's primary contribution is not classification performance---where multi-detector ensembles match or marginally exceed it---but the decomposition of depth signal into location-sensitive and shape-stable components, a structural distinction absent from both binary detection methods and model collapse analyses (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
141	
```

Cross-generator binary F1: SD\\,=\\,0.028, IQR\\,=\\,[0.692, 0.714]. $^{\\dagger}$3-class F1 carries upward bias because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}); independent-seed controls confirm the depth signal (F1\\,=\\,0.680; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-control}).}\n\\label{tab:logo-results}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Differential Depth Response}\n\\label{sec:results-differential}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth. \\textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\\rho| > 0.59$). \\textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (blue for drifting, amber for stationary). This dichotomy defines the differential depth response (DDR).}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nNot all distributional properties respond equally to recursive generation.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals a striking dichotomy that holds across all eight generators: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat.\nWe term this asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR).\nThe primary partition is binary: $k{=}2$ clustering in standardized $(|\\rho|, d)$ space yields silhouette\\,=\\,0.660 (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}), cleanly separating three \\emph{drifting} features ($|\\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) from four \\emph{non-drifting} ones ($d \\leq 0.55$); higher $k$ degrades cluster quality ($k{=}3$: silhouette\\,=\\,0.532).\nA domain-informed refinement resolves entropy std as a sole \\emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.55$, $|\\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; CV shift $<$3\\% vs.\\ $>$15\\% for drifting features).\nConvergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures converge on this partition, confirming that the location/shape asymmetry reflects distributional structure rather than feature-selection artifact (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}, Quantification).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values. Drifting features (blue; $|\\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) show strong monotonic depth sensitivity; non-drifting features remain stable. Entropy std (wine; $d{=}0.55$) is an intermediate member; non-drifting features (amber; $d < 0.10$) show negligible drift. Entropy min ($|\\rho|{=}0.40$ pooled; gen-0/gen-1 sensitive) and tail decay (TOST 4/8) are boundary features within this group.}\n\\label{fig:feature-drift}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPooled class-balanced Spearman correlations with depth confirm the binary split (Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}):\ndrifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\\rho = -0.64$, TTR $\\rho = -0.63$, entropy mean $\\rho = -0.60$),\nwhile non-drifting features remain near-zero (tail decay $|\\rho| = 0.04$; per-depth distributions in Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-distributions}).\nThe six-fold magnitude gap between drifting ($|\\rho| > 0.60$) and non-drifting features ($|\\rho| \\leq 0.16$) defines the DDR partition.\nWithin the non-drifting group, entropy std ($|\\rho| = 0.16$, $d{=}0.55$) shows a substantial level shift despite low monotonicity, distinguishing it from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$); this refinement is stable across $d$ thresholds in $[0.56, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Quantification.}\nConvergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures confirm the split.\nCohen's $d$ \\cite{cohen1988statistical} shows a ${\\geq}7\\times$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and near-stationary features ($d \\leq 0.08$); KS statistics, Glass's $\\Delta$, Cliff's $\\delta$, and CV shift all corroborate (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\nFour features fall cleanly into drifting or non-drifting groups.\nEntropy min and tail decay are \\emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1 ($|\\rho|{=}0.40$); tail decay passes TOST for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).\nEntropy std ($d{=}0.55$, $|\\rho|{=}0.16$) is the sole intermediate member.\n\\paragraph{Validation.}\nThe drifting/non-drifting boundary is not manually set: it emerges from $k{=}2$ clustering in standardized $(|\\rho|, d)$ space and is invariant across threshold choices $\\varepsilon \\in [0.10, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\nPer-generator TOST equivalence testing ($\\varepsilon{=}|d|{<}0.20$, following Cohen's~\\citeyearpar{cohen1988statistical} conventional threshold; gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8 pass) and entropy max (6/8); tail decay passes for only 4/8, with $|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$ spanning the boundary.\nA permutation test ($n{=}10{,}000$) confirms the three-vs-four split is non-random ($p{=}0.030$; fixed-label silhouette\\,=\\,0.660 vs.\\ null mean\\,=\\,0.46); exhaustive enumeration of all $\\binom{7}{3}{=}35$ possible splits ranks this partition first (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\nGenerator-split consistency further validates the partition: the drifting/non-drifting boundary persists across all eight hold-out generators.\nReclassifying either boundary feature as intermediate preserves the core drifting/non-drifting split and leaves classification unchanged.\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThe full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.773) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \\approx 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation-table}).\nPCA confirms the three drifting features share a single latent dimension (92.3\\% variance; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}); replacing them with PC1 costs only 1.1\\,pp, confirming that DDR's contribution is identifying \\emph{which} distributional properties drift, not classification per se.\nTTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are both retained for marginal LOFO gains despite feature masking \\cite{strobl_conditional_2008} (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}\nAlternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable LOGO F1 (spread 0.013; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}), bounding the same-family affinity effect; depth features are robust to reference model choice, though residual same-family proximity may inflate Pythia-family F1 by up to 1.3\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}\nAll four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-9.3$ to $-26.0$\\,pp binary F1); low-temperature improvement is family-dependent ($+7$--$9$\\,pp for Pythia/OLMo at $t{=}0.5$, negligible for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}).\nContinuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\\,=\\,0.781), with cross-prompt-type transfer confirming shared signal (LOPTO F1\\,=\\,0.753; Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}); the DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures despite absolute effect-size compression (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\nExisting detection methods target binary real-vs-synthetic classification; we test whether their continuous scores incidentally encode recursive depth by repurposing four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Feat.} & \\textbf{3-cl F1} & \\textbf{Bin F1} & \\textbf{ms/text} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4-det.\\ + surface     & 6 & \\textbf{0.795} & \\textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\\\\n4-detector ens.\\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\\\\n7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \\textbf{9.7} \\\\\n\\midrule\nAvg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\\\\nFastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\\\\nLD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\\\\nBinoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled evaluation; slightly higher than per-generator macro average in Table~\\ref{tab:logo-results}). Identical XGBoost configuration. 7-feature: one 410M model (905\\,MB); 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B params (2{,}868\\,MB). Individual detection scores (N/A) share the reference-model forward pass; per-method timing is not separately profiled.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\\ inference cost in ms/text (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M reference model) matches multi-detector ensembles (two models, 1.4B total) at 2.4$\\times$ lower cost; individual detectors vary widely when repurposed for depth.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe 7-feature ensemble surpasses all individual baselines in 3-class F1 ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,6.7--36.3\\,pp; Figure~\\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) and the pure 4-detector ensemble in binary F1 (0.705 vs.\\ 0.700).\nAdding DDR's surface features to the four detectors yields the highest binary F1 (0.708), marginally above the 7-feature set at 2.4$\\times$ the inference cost.\nThis result underscores that DDR's primary contribution is not classification performance---where multi-detector ensembles match or marginally exceed it---but the decomposition of depth signal into location-sensitive and shape-stable components, a structural distinction absent from both binary detection methods and model collapse analyses (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n",
```json
      "numLines": 141,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 141
    }
  },
```

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
5	\label{sec:results-classification}
6	
7	The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.762 (95\% CI [0.758, 0.766]; $N$\,=\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}), with cross-generator F1 spread of 9.1\,pp capturing generator selection uncertainty.
8	Because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}), gen-0 classification is near-perfect (per-class F1\,$\approx$\,0.94), inflating the 3-class metric.
9	We therefore adopt \textbf{leak-free binary F1} (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+)\,=\,0.694 (CI [0.688, 0.700]) as the primary metric throughout: it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing.
10	Per-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B), with the primary difficulty concentrated at the gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary (Appendix~\ref{app:confusion}).
11	
12	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
13	Cross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield binary F1\,=\,0.830 (95\% CI [.818, .841]) under 5-generator LOGO.\footnote{5-generator subset (Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B); generator-level LOGO with bootstrap CIs ($n$\,=\,2{,}000). Not directly comparable to 8-generator LOGO due to differing subsets.}
14	Single-generator transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}) confirms the signal is not a mixed-chain artifact: classifiers trained on one generator transfer to all others with mean binary F1\,=\,0.810; LOFO yields 0.686.
15	A scale effect emerges: Pythia achieves the highest F1 (0.718--0.724) while larger models degrade (Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (cross-reference spread $\leq$0.013; \S\ref{sec:results-differential}); cross-domain signal persists with variation (Appendix~\ref{app:domain}).
16	An end-to-end QLoRA classifier achieves 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 under 6-generator LOGO ($+$11.6\,pp vs.\ the same-subset XGBoost baseline of 0.789; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that richer token-level representations capture additional depth signal; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 within-model (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the empirical decomposition---not the specific classifier---as DDR's primary contribution.
17	
18	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
19	Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\,=\,0.724 (vs.\ 0.770 unmatched); a random-subsample control confirms the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
20	
21	\paragraph{Seed-sharing control.}
22	Seed-level demeaning (F1\,=\,0.701) and an independent-seed experiment with disjoint seed pools (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}) both confirm the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.
23	
24	\begin{table}[t]
25	\centering
26	\footnotesize
27	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
28	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
29	\toprule
30	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{3-cl F1}$^{\dagger}$ \\
31	\midrule
32	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 2.8B & \textbf{0.724} & [.707, .742] & 0.798 \\
33	Pythia-1B       & Pythia  & 1.0B & 0.718 & [.700, .735] & \textbf{0.806} \\
34	Qwen3-1.7B     & Qwen    & 1.7B & 0.709 & [.693, .726] & 0.772 \\
35	Llama-3.2-3B   & Llama   & 3.2B & 0.697 & [.680, .713] & 0.775 \\
36	OLMo-2-1B      & OLMo    & 1.5B & 0.695 & [.679, .711] & 0.781 \\
37	Qwen3-8B       & Qwen    & 8.2B & 0.692 & [.674, .708] & 0.714 \\
38	Qwen3-0.6B     & Qwen    & 0.6B & 0.691 & [.673, .709] & 0.780 \\
39	Mistral-7B     & Mistral & 7.0B & 0.633 & [.617, .648] & 0.682 \\
40	\midrule
41	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textbf{Average (8-gen LOGO)}} & \textbf{0.694} & [.688, .700] & \textbf{0.762} \\
42	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textbf{LOFO (family-level)}} & 0.686 & --- & --- \\
43	\bottomrule
44	\end{tabular}}
45	\caption{8-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\% CIs, $n$\,=\,1000). Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is the primary metric; it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing. Cross-generator binary F1: SD\,=\,0.028, IQR\,=\,[0.692, 0.714]. $^{\dagger}$3-class F1 carries upward bias because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}); independent-seed controls confirm the depth signal (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}).}
46	\label{tab:logo-results}
47	\end{table}
48	
49	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
50	\label{sec:results-differential}
51	
52	\begin{figure*}[t]
53	\centering
54	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}
55	\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth. \textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\rho| > 0.59$). \textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (blue for drifting, amber for stationary). This dichotomy defines the differential depth response (DDR).}
56	\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}
57	\end{figure*}
58	
59	Not all distributional properties respond equally to recursive generation.
60	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals a striking dichotomy that holds across all eight generators: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat.
61	We term this asymmetry the \emph{differential depth response} (DDR).
62	The primary partition is binary: $k{=}2$ clustering in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space yields silhouette\,=\,0.660 (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}), cleanly separating three \emph{drifting} features ($|\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) from four \emph{non-drifting} ones ($d \leq 0.55$); higher $k$ degrades cluster quality ($k{=}3$: silhouette\,=\,0.532).
63	A domain-informed refinement resolves entropy std as a sole \emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; CV shift $<$3\% vs.\ $>$15\% for drifting features).
64	Convergence across parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures converge on this partition, confirming that the location/shape asymmetry reflects distributional structure rather than feature-selection artifact (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}, Quantification).
65	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WJpTUMEmuZh2aETqumEpdB`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\subsection{AI-Generated Text Detection}
5	\label{sec:related-detection}
6	
7	The detection literature spans statistical tests \cite{gltr,grover,release_strategies}, zero-shot scoring \cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,dna_gpt}, surprisal diversity \cite{diveye}, geometric invariants \cite{intrinsic_dim_detection}, and learned classifiers \cite{ghostbuster,feat_aug_transformer,reliable_detection,ld_score}; see \citet{wu_survey_2025} and \citet{jawahar_survey} for surveys.
8	RAID \cite{raid} benchmarks binary detection across generators and attacks; we extend the task from binary to ordinal depth estimation.
9	Several recent efforts move beyond the binary boundary---collaboration roles \cite{beyond_binary}, joint detection-attribution \cite{two_birds}, creator-editor modes \cite{beyond_final_actor}, multi-generator attribution \cite{semeval2024_task8}---but all taxonomize by \emph{source} or \emph{production mode}, not recursive generation \emph{depth}; repurposing their scores as depth predictors reveals high cross-generator variance (binary F1\,=\,0.52--0.71; Table~\ref{tab:baselines}) \cite{paraphrase_evades,detection_possibilities}.
10	Our contribution is orthogonal: ordinal depth classification \cite{gutierrez_ordinal_survey,coral_ordinal,kasa_ordinality_nlp} as a task, and the finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance where individual detection scores show high variance \cite{uchendu_attribution,munir_looking_glass}.
11	
12	\subsection{Model Collapse and Iterative Generation}
13	\label{sec:related-collapse}
14	
15	Self-consuming generative models progressively lose distributional coverage \cite{self_consuming_mad,curse_of_recursion,seddik_synthetic_data}, though data mixing mitigates collapse \cite{model_collapse_avoidable,strong_model_collapse}.
16	These studies address \emph{training-loop} degradation where parameter updates erode tail coverage; indeed, \citet{schaeffer_collapse_position} identify eight distinct definitions of ``collapse,'' most tied to retraining dynamics.
17	We study \emph{inference chains} where parameters are fixed and only the input distribution evolves.
18	Two concurrent works are most directly relevant: \citet{broken_telephone} analyze paraphrasing and translation chains, documenting progressive information loss, while \citet{markovian_chains} demonstrate attractor dynamics that bound distinguishable depth to approximately three classes; \citet{attractor_cycles} provide complementary evidence that LLM outputs converge to cyclic attractor states under repeated generation.
19	We study \emph{non-paraphrastic continuation} chains, where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal, and offer a complementary decomposition: where these works characterize \emph{that} and \emph{why} convergence occurs, DDR isolates \emph{what} converges (NLL location) from what is preserved (NLL shape), enabling ordinal classification without per-generator calibration.
20	The independent convergence of all three approaches on ${\sim}$3 distinguishable depth classes---via Markovian dynamics \cite{markovian_chains}, information-theoretic loss \cite{broken_telephone}, and distributional feature compression (this work)---points to a shared resolution ceiling in recursive text generation (Table~\ref{tab:iterative-comparison} in Appendix~\ref{app:iterative-comparison} provides a structured comparison).
21	
22	\subsection{Vocabulary Diversity and Stylometry}
23	\label{sec:related-diversity}
24	
25	Declining lexical diversity in LLM text is documented in both training \cite{curious_decline} and deployment settings \cite{writing_diversity}; MAUVE \cite{mauve} provides a divergence-based metric for quantifying this distributional gap.
26	Our surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) are classical stylometric measures \cite{stamatatos_survey,koppel_authorship}; we repurpose them from generator identity \cite{uchendu_attribution} to the depth dimension, where their monotonic decline under top-$p$ sampling \cite{nucleus_sampling} constitutes a drifting component of DDR.
27	
28	\subsection{Data Quality and Provenance}
29	\label{sec:related-provenance}
30	
31	Scalable data curation relies on quality signals \cite{dsir,qurating,ccnet}; generation depth offers a complementary provenance axis.
32	Proactive watermarking \cite{watermark_llm,synthid_text,aaronson_watermark} embeds identifiable patterns but degrades across multi-step chains \cite{chainwash}, motivating passive approaches.
33	Our NLL-based analysis connects to \citet{min_k_percent} (probability-tail membership inference) but extends from binary membership to full distributional decomposition for ordinal depth.
34	\citet{koike_mia_detection} formalize this connection from the opposite direction, showing that detection scores implicitly perform membership inference, underscoring the shared statistical basis of detection and provenance.
35	
```

### Assistant

_source line 2502 · 2026-05-21T06:57:35.439Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
The introduction from earlier:

```
\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:introduction}

AI text detection asks a single binary question---\emph{human or machine?}---and recent methods answer it with increasing reliability \cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.
Yet this framing elides a richer structure emerging on the open web: as LLM outputs re-enter training corpora \cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \emph{recursive depths}---an estimated 7--17\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}---and each generation cycle pushes distributional properties further from both the human source and the first-generation predecessor.


```

Now I'm moving into the core problem: while most deployed systems use instruction-tuned models, the base models are what matter for synthetic data and recursive augmentation, so that's where I'm focusing. The key insight is that knowing the generation depth matters for understanding data provenance and model collapse, but existing methods only give us a binary answer. Distributional compression happens predictably across recursive generations, so I'm proposing depth fingerprinting to classify text by how many times it's been recursively generated rather than just whether it's human or machine.

Testing this across eight different base models of varying sizes and architectures, I'm finding something interesting: the central tendency of the distribution—things like mean entropy and lexical diversity—shifts consistently as generation depth increases, but the shape of the distribution—the extremes and tail behavior—stays remarkably stable. This differential depth response appears to be a fundamental property of how autoregressive sampling works, distinct from the kind of tail erosion you see in model collapse.

```
\begin{enumerate}[
```
nosep,leftmargin=*]
    \item \textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Clustering-based validation ($k{=}2$, silhouette\,=\,0.660) confirms that inference-chain drift separates into a drifting location component ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and a stable shape component ($d < 0.10$). Where prior work documents \emph{what converges}---diversity loss \cite{curious_decline}, attractor dynamics \cite{markovian_chains}---DDR identifies \emph{what is preserved}: shape invariance provides the cross-generator reference needed for depth detection without per-generator calibration.
    \item \textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts
```

The decomposition validates that drift splits cleanly into location and shape components, with shape remaining stable across generators—this invariance is what enables depth detection without calibration per model. We're also formalizing depth classification as an ordinal task and building a 37K-text evaluation dataset.

Now I'm setting up the evaluation framework with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation across eight generators spanning five model families at different scales, then mapping where the DDR-based approach succeeds and fails across temperature, prompt variations, instruction tuning, and model size.

Checking the introduction against R38's concerns—the "formalize" issue is resolved by using "decompose" and "identifies" instead, and the framing correctly positions DDR as decomposing known distributional compression rather than discovering something entirely new. The title and "fingerprinting" metaphor hold up fine given the abstract's explicit mention of three effective depth classes.

Looking at the overall contribution trajectory, the paper has solid analytical grounding with the DDR decomposition and strong empirical results showing 6.7–36.3 percentage point improvements over baseline detection methods.

The core strengths are the controlled experimental setup across 37K texts and 8 generators, the LOGO evaluation protocol, and thorough ablations, though the moderate F1 score and base-model-only scope are acknowledged limitations. If the greedy/top-k stability experiment confirms the shape hypothesis holds, that directly addresses the recurring concern about robustness and could meaningfully shift the evaluation. Given the four-day deadline, I should focus on letting the current work stand rather than pursuing additional experiments.

Now I'm reviewing the Limitations section to see how it frames these constraints for reviewers.
````

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	All eight generators are base (pretrained) models; instruction-tuned variants show substantially degraded depth signal (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), and extending the method to RLHF-aligned models---which dominate real-world text---requires addressing confounds from length regulation and safety-driven distribution shaping.
5	Our largest generator is 8.2B parameters; frontier models (70B+) remain untested and their higher fluency may further compress feature margins.
6	
7	The evaluation assumes a fixed decoding regime (temperature 0.7, nucleus $p{=}0.9$); cross-temperature transfer degrades when test-time temperature exceeds training-time settings (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-temp}).
8	All seed texts are English Wikipedia excerpts, and the reference model (Pythia-410M) has English-centric pretraining, limiting non-English applicability.
9	
10	Depth resolution is bounded at three classes (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+); finer-grained classification (5-class) yields F1\,$\approx$\,0.50 with depths 2--4 mutually indistinguishable (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}).
11	Finally, our cross-model chain and simulated editing experiments (\S\ref{sec:discussion}) represent controlled laboratory settings; the gap to real-world post-processing pipelines remains untested.
12	
```
